ParlaMint-DK_20150428130245	At first sight, it may be difficult to imagine why a proposal to adjust plant cover should be able to trigger a speech at the third reading too, but I really think this bill is really, really interesting. And if you look at it a little bit historically, it also marks a low point in Danish environmental policy, which I do not think we should dwell on a little bit. It was the case in 2009 that then the then VK government implemented the agreement with the Danish People's Party, which was called "Green Growth." At that time, the opposition at that time, some of which are currently in government, agreed that it was completely inadequate from the point of view of the environment, and it was promised that after a forthcoming election, when a new government and a new majority came into being, it would be taken into the other hand. If it had now been said at the time that a Social Democratic radical government would enter into an agreement with the Left, Conservative, Danish People's Party and Liberal Alliance to remove one of the few effective environmental measures in Green Growth, then it would probably have been accused of being both overly negative, skeptical, black-eyed and all sorts of other things. Nevertheless, this is what happens here today if this bill is adopted: one of the few effective environmental measures adopted under the VK government is removed and, moreover, in an agreement with the Danish People's Party. It is becoming even more curious because, at a time when everyone agrees that a targeted effort must be made and targeted regulation of the environmental impact of agriculture, this is where we carefully go in and remove targeted regulation. We know that this has had a really, very good effect both in terms of dealing with nitrogen leaching and in terms of solving some of the climate challenges we face. You go in and remove this targeted effort, and instead you agree that we need a little more of that more general. In other words, you remove something targeted that works and replace it with something general that you know to have a much smaller effect, but you do. That is contrary to what was said in the report by the Nature and Agriculture Commission, which said that we should make things more targeted and less general, but here we are doing the opposite. The truth is also that the only reason why we could agree on this in the so-called growth negotiations is beyond the particular challenge that it always is when it comes to growth negotiations, because then it ends up being the Finance Minister who, together with the Left President, has to agree on things, and then, when it comes to the environment and agriculture, it can be marked by a little superficial knowledge of what is working and what does not work was that it was important for the Left, who brought it here, to achieve a result where environmental control and environmental efforts were weakened and then gave agriculture better conditions of competition. Whether it was, by the way, a meaningless way of doing so, according to everything that has been said in the past, did not matter. The Nature and Agriculture Commission, all of which agreed to come up with a very good report here, was really inspiring to the further work that we did not say that we should weaken the protection of nature and the environment. You said that there was a need for enhanced natural and environmental protection, and then you had to make it targeted in order to allow room to also provide opportunities for agriculture to earn money. But here we choose to go in and weaken our efforts for nature and the environment, and then we do so by removing targeted action. I have to say that everything is turned upside down, because you had to replace general bets with targeted bets, and because you didn't think you were ready with the bets that were needed to make a targeted effort, you chose to do the opposite. The consequence of this is that we have here a legislative proposal which means clear deterioration in relation to the protection of nature and the environment and in relation to nitrogen emissions and which results in clear deterioration for the climate. Now you shouldn't talk shit about the government without talking good about the government too. After all, it's nice with the government that when they've made such bad deals with the right, they can make some good deals with Enhedslisten, where you repair a little bit of the damage, and it's also done in this area. It is just before the government in the environmental field often ends up coming out with only a small minus, because after they have made a large minus with the right wing making an almost equal plus with Enhedslisten and SF. It is, of course, sympathetic and is probably also an argument for having a social democratic government rather than a left-wing government. Because they only make the bad and never come to us to repair the damage. So, of course, you have to take that with you. That is the way it is in relation to this bill, and I would have liked to have said something about that at second reading, but I did not get a chance to actually sneak in on one little really smart thing, because the minister had been smuggled in something that was about if some farmers did something sensible to protect the environment, they could be rewarded for it, and that meant that some other farmers who did not do anything to help the environment had to pay a little bit to it. However, it was vitally important for the Left to have it removed and, fortunately, they could point out that in the growth agreement they had made no room for this kind of environmental nonsense, so it disappeared at second reading, where we tried to maintain it, but where we had to face up to the fact that there was no possibility of maintaining just the slightest improvement on this draft law. It was • and I think it should be remembered at first reading that Mr. Erling Bonnesen made one of these touching speeches on Danish agriculture, where it was clear that not a single farmer in this country should be subject to a single additional requirement, even if that additional requirement is a consequence of the better application of other farmers. It simply has to be so that the Left can be involved in everything moving back environmentally and naturally, and the exciting thing here is that part of the decline is to clean up after the green excrement, which evidently according to the Left's own view was in Green Growth. I find it sad that this bill seems to be adopted. I think it would be possible to make coherent agreements with solutions to both environmental, natural and agricultural issues, which were much more forward-looking and which gave us much more opportunity in the future to have a thriving food business in this country, instead of the backward-to-time green growth policy that the Left stands for in this debate. Unfortunately, it has to be said that the government has chosen to take a rather shaky course instead of seeking a majority, where one could have a policy that created a proper future for Danish food production as well as for the environment and nature in Denmark, and that is why we have this really, really bad bill. Now I have been with you for many years, so I will not try to make any appeal for the Socialists and Radicals to come to their senses at the last minute, because I know that does not happen, but it would have been very good if you had thought a little earlier, because we would not have got into this unfortunate situation.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428140208	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it's Mrs. Özlem Sara Cekic for the SF.
ParlaMint-DK_20150428140213	In fact, I think it is an incredibly happy day today, because there is such a large, broad majority for us to look at this legislation, especially because this bill in relation to our mentally ill citizens states that the purpose of psychiatric treatment in Denmark is to recover. There is, for the first time, an objective clause in the legislation, which stresses that the aim is not to be treated or forcibly withdrawn, but to recover. It is an entirely paradigm shift, in relation to whether you believe that people with mental illness can recover or whether you basically believe that they are chronically ill for the rest of their lives. That is why, of course, I also hope that the legislation can help change the culture in many departments, so that you actually believe that the individual can come or can learn to live with his or her illness. Another thing that I think is quite historic is that with this bill we are now stressing the importance of relatives. We know today that when you go in and involve the relatives of course if the patient so wishes, the probability is that the patient will be forced into hospital, remanded and forcibly medicated actually very small, compared to if you do not involve the relatives. There are over a million Danes who are relatives of a mentally ill person in today's Denmark, and the vast majority of them really want to continue to be a part of the citizen's life, and the vast majority of them are also a resource. Unfortunately, however, a great many of them experience that you meet them as a limitation, meet them as an annoyance, rather than meet them as someone who actually wants the best for their relatives. The third point I would like to emphasise is that there will be better supervision in relation to forced belt fixings. Now it is the case that if there is a person who is going to be subjected to coercion, then much more often there must be supervision, so we are 100% sure that coercion is the last resort. Unfortunately, there are a great many of the last few years of investigations which show that it is not always the case that coercion is the last resort, but unfortunately also some places have become part of a treatment, and that is not the purpose at all. I would have liked to see some things we could have done better. As the rapporteur for the Danish People's Party also stressed, it would have looked good on us if we had also been able to ensure legal certainty with this law for children and young people who are in psychiatry, especially children and especially those who are under 15 years of age. I still do not think it is parents' responsibility when a child is so ill that it is estimated that that child should be treated with coercion. I believe that it is a medical assessment that needs to be taken, also because many parents say that it is really difficult to stand in the situation where you are the one who decides whether the child should be forcibly medicated, forced or forcibly detained. They want to be allowed to remain parents. I am sorry that we did not manage to achieve that ambition too. Now we'll see what happens. There are two things we have emphasised by the SF which have been very difficult for us. One is up-to-date fixations, as you can see in the fuse. We voted against it at the time, and we have also written in the comments in the report that we believe that it sends an unfortunate signal that we are making a forced law to be able to halve that is the ambition, that is the goal • while at the same time somewhere else in Denmark we have a hospital where up-to-date patients wear these belts and look like Guantanamo prisoners. Actually, I don't think it's okay with that patient group. The second is outpatient coercion, where we have run a trial period of 4 years, where you go in and say: there are citizens we cannot reach and who, despite several attempts, continue to get out of their medical care. When we entered into the negotiations, it was important for us that this was not used as an opportunity to say that now there was no need to do an outreach work. Therefore, a number of criteria were put in place that there should be outreach psychoseteams, one should repeatedly try to create a contact and there should be forced admissions. And now this first evaluation shows that neither patients nor those who work with it think it's a good idea. The Minister really wants us to continue for four more years, and there we have said: let us do it. But it is a little strange that a project is being carried out which will subsequently have to be evaluated and where you come to the conclusion that it is not very good, and then you say: we give it 4 more years. I do not think the result will be different in four years' time, because I hope that we do what we can to prevent patients from being discharged into nothing. That's what the vast majority of people actually say they're being discharged into nothing, and that the municipal psychiatry isn't very good at catching these patients and helping them move on, but that they fall between two chairs. That is why the call is also from here to the municipalities and to the hospitals: from the legislative point of view we have done what we could easily have a higher level of ambition, but we must try to halve the pressure. Now it is up to you also to ensure that there is cooperation between you. Now it is up to the municipalities and hospitals not to turn it into a sectoral war, but actually to work for the best for the patient. Whether the citizen is hospitalized or the citizen has been discharged, it requires coordination, and I hope that this will also be a part of it. I am really, really happy about that, and I would like to thank the Minister for the fact that this bill actually comes into being. This is the first time in so many years that there will be a revolution in psychiatry, where you enter politically from Christiansborg and set a goal that we want to halve coercion, that we want a psychiatry based on dialogue, on equality and equality, and that's why you do this. We can always discuss where the level of ambition should lie, but I think it is extremely positive that, for the first time and with such a broad majority, we should now go in and say that people who have mental illness must meet with equal dignity; we should ask how they feel and we should hear what they want. ParlaMint-DK_20150428144334	The debate is open. First, the Minister for Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20150428144405	The motion for a resolution we are debating here gives us the opportunity to discuss what I consider to be an important issue, namely the protection of employees against violence and threats. Let that be said straight away: I, the government, recognise that employees who are subjected to violence at work can face a difficult dilemma if they are to report to a citizen they are caring for on a daily basis. I would therefore like to acknowledge that the parties address the problem raised in the motion for a resolution. Violence and threats are unfortunately part of the harsh reality in some jobs in Denmark. I would like to start by briefly sketching out the rules on the prevention of violence and threats. It follows from the legislation on working conditions that work at all stages must be planned, organised and carried out so that it is fully safe in terms of safety and health. This means, among other things, that employees must be protected from physical and psychological violence and threats from citizens, patients and relatives. And it is the employer's duty to handle the task and to prevent threats of violence. Having said that, we in the government have also made a number of efforts to prevent violence and threats. As recently as Easter, we reached a broad agreement to further strengthen health and safety efforts. The agreement means, among other things, that we are focusing more on those companies where we know that the problems are greatest. This also applies to jobs where employees are exposed to violence and threats. In addition, the Danish Labour Inspectorate carried out the supervisory work with a focus on mental health and safety, including, of course, violence and threats. The government has also initiated a violence prevention package targeted at sectors where there is an increased risk of being exposed to violence and threats. Furthermore, with a change in the Working Conditions Act, the government has now also committed employers to preventing violence and threats to employees outside working hours. Finally, I would like to mention that, as of April 2015, the government will make it possible to register violence against employees when reporting accidents at work. As something new, it must also be stated if an accident at work is the result of violence and threats when it is reported. a significantly better registration. Today, people who have been victims of violence are guaranteed by the Labour Insurance Act and the Victims' Compensation Act. The Work Damage Insurance Act provides employees with cover for treatment costs, compensation for permanent but also compensation for loss of occupational and working capacity. If it can be demonstrated that the violence has taken place, compensation may be paid. In addition, employees who are victims of violence also have the possibility of obtaining compensation under the victim compensation law. The Offer Compensation Act provides compensation for health expenses, lost earnings, pain and pain, permanent but, loss of earning capacity, torture and abuse, etc. Compensation under the victim compensation law requires, as a general rule, a police report. Employees who have then been subjected to violence at work can thus, under the rules in force, be compensated to a large extent for their losses. However, the government recognises the concerns presented in the motion for a resolution, in particular the ethical dilemma of having to report to a citizen who is taken care of every day. Some employees who have been victims of violence choose not to notify or sue the injured party as it may challenge the future relationship between the employee and the injured party. And I can actually understand that position. That is why I believe it is also the responsibility of employers to ensure that the abuses are reported to the police so that the employee is not put in this dilemma. The employer has a considerable responsibility to ensure, on the one hand, that there is a response to violence and, on the other hand, that employees who have been subjected to violence or threats receive the necessary help and support to move forward. I recognise the issues that the motion for a resolution will look at. At the moment, together with the social partners, we are in fact discussing the possibility of modernising the whole system of occupational injury. These discussions are based on the recommendations of the Committee of Experts, which we received back in the field of occupational injury in December 2014. The Committee of Experts shall have, inter alia: recommended the introduction of the possibility of compensation for pain and pain and compensation for loss of earnings in the work injury system. If those recommendations are followed, the problems of compensation for people who have been subjected to violence at work will be significantly reduced. I therefore think it is most natural that we should now, at first, wait for the discussions of the parties that are so well under way, and afterwards we can consider politically whether this has become good enough or how we deal with it. That is why the government cannot recommend the motion for a resolution at this very time, but clearly recognizes the issues involved and hopes that the work being done with the parties on new legislation on occupational injuries will also help to solve the problem here.

However, if you have suffered permanent but or occupational loss, you must, like others who have suffered occupational injuries, have your compensation under the Labour Damage Act on an objective basis. In addition, the vast majority of employees who are at risk of exposure to violence at their workplace will typically be covered by the civil servant law to receive wages during illness. The employees are therefore already relatively well covered if the accident is out. Although I have a great deal of sympathy for the aim of the resolution, we must carefully consider whether putting forward long-term claims, which will typically only deal with small amounts in addition to occupational injury insurance, is really in the interests of the individual. Much research points to the fact that the longer you are in a process of replacement, the more difficult it is to get back into the labour market. And this is definitely not beneficial to the health and quality of life of the individual. In addition, a large and comprehensive report was prepared in December 2014 with recommendations for a new work injury system by the Jørgen Søndergaard Committee. With these arguments, the Left cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428160116	First of all, thank you for being able to deal with the two motions for resolutions together, which also makes sense. It may well be said that this question has to be said gradually to be such an old acquaintance on the agenda here in the Folketing. This is an issue that has been discussed in connection with the financial laws of recent years, in the spring of B 93, and most recently in the Employment Committee we have had a conciliation where it has also been discussed. The government's position on working clauses is very clear: we are in favour of this. We see working clauses as an important element in the effort to prevent social dumping. Since the government came to power, it has been our top priority to put an end to social dumping. This is among other things. have been made possible by the Finance Act agreements we have concluded with the Enhedslisten. I'd like to sign for that. The government, together with Enhedslisten and SF, has already taken a large number of initiatives to ensure that we continue to have fair ground rules on the Danish labour market. We have increased the use of clauses, among others. by requiring state companies to use working clauses in all public procurement of construction projects by removing the threshold of 37.5 million. kr. and by increasing the use of work clauses in both municipalities and regions. More than 670 million have been set aside. kr. to strengthen the authorities' efforts from 2012 onwards, and we have increased control in the workplace, where we have, of course, just strengthened cooperation between the police, the SKAT and the Labour Inspectorate. We have tightened up the requirements for registration of foreign companies and we have strengthened the sanctions; we have strengthened the efforts in the cleaning industry with control and the introduction of responsibilities; and we have allocated funds to combat and control illegal goods transport. The use of working clauses is one of the effective means of combating social dumping. Therefore, and precisely for this reason, the government and Danske Regioner and KL agreed during the economic negotiations last year that working clauses in municipalities and regions should be applied to all works contracts and other tender contracts where appropriate. In my view, this has actually worked. On 12 March this year, the KL published a study of, inter alia: the use of work clauses in the municipalities. This study shows, among other things, that 97 per cent of the population in the United Kingdom are in the Netherlands. of the municipalities use work clauses or consider adopting guidelines for their use, and that 83 per cent. of the municipalities often apply work clauses in works contracts. I think that somewhere it proves that we are on the right track, that it has been possible to increase the use of work clauses in municipalities by voluntary means. Nor do I think that it will be a great surprise for the proposers when we say in the government that we do not think it is the right way to impose working clauses on municipalities and regions. The fact is that if by law we impose working clauses on municipalities and regions, we will move exactly into an area which we traditionally leave to the social partners. If the commitment of municipalities and regions is to make sense, it must be clear from the law what working clauses should look like. If we were to legislate, we would have to state clearly in the law which part of the salary is to be included in the statement of salary for the employees employed to carry out the work in question. Salary is not something we normally legislate about in Denmark. This is a matter for the social partners. I would like to summarise how the government views the two motions for resolutions. It will not be legally possible to oblige municipalities and regions to use working clauses through circulars. It would be legally possible to oblige municipalities and regions to use working clauses through legislation on this, but the government believes that this is a bad idea because it will require legislation on pay conditions, and thus it would not be in line with the Danish model. In fact, we believe, and we also believe that it is documented that the way of volunteering works. So we cannot support the motions for resolutions, but we support, as everyone knows, the fight against social dumping.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428161550	Thank you, President. Today we are discussing B 85 and B 155 on working clauses in all public contracts. After all, it is becoming an annual habit for us to meet in Parliament and discuss proposals on this subject, and for those who remember my rapporteur's speech from last year I can see that some of the rapporteurs who are sitting here are the same as the previous year, what I am saying will probably not come as the big surprise, because the authors of the motion have found a way of resubmitting their proposals, but the Left has not changed their views on the matter since last time. I hope, of course, that this is not too much a disappointment for the proposers. The left is against social dumping. We just do not believe that the solution is legislation. It sounds sympathetic, at least immediately, when the proposers want to strengthen the Danish collective agreement model and combat social dumping. And it also sounds quite attractive when the proposals aim to ensure that Danish tax dollars are not used for contracts with private companies which undercut the contractual conditions. However, the problem with the proposal arises when one tries to legislate one more time out of the challenges. After all, the Danish model is precisely about the social partners, employers and employees, agreements, working conditions in terms of wages, working hours, etc., in an agreement. Therefore, I do not think it is surprising that it is a step in the wrong direction when the proposers want to regulate the labour market. An interesting question could be whether you want to do as you do in Germany and to table a resolution tomorrow on a fixed minimum wage. I do not think the proposers would like to do so, but we can, of course, discuss it later. It will then be just a matter of time before we have such a stranglehold-like relationship, where politicians in this Parliament are interfering in everything that is going on in the Danish labour market. The Left is not in favour of this. In addition, we believe that the proposals have a number of negative consequences for companies that are burdened with excessive burdens, and chain liability is problematic for small and medium-sized enterprises because they will have difficulty competing with the larger ones so that it will distort competition. In short, chain liability will exclude a large number of small and medium-sized enterprises from offering public services, since they simply do not have the resources to meet the high transaction costs which the proposers' proposals will entail. I am quite sure that the bill for this unequal competitive situation, with additional bureaucratic costs, will end up where it is customary, namely with consumers and therefore also with citizens and taxpayers. The Left simply will not be involved in this. At the same time, it is also worth thinking the idea just a little bit further, and I have done so compared to last year, so what I am saying now is perhaps more surprising. If we start to discriminate against companies through different rules in Denmark, other EU countries can do exactly the same. And this means that skilled Danish companies that are competitive at home will find it difficult to become competitive abroad, as the rules will be different. Of course, the work in Denmark must be carried out within the limits laid down in the law, but it is primarily the responsibility of the various authorities to check that the law is observed. I also believe that only the largest companies actually have the resources, the ability and the knowledge necessary to control their subcontractors down to the last detail. And that will, of course, be the consequence of chain responsibility. I also think it is worth taking into account the uncertainty as to whether the proposal is contrary to EU law. I mentioned that last year too. It is doubtful whether the State can impose on regions and municipalities the application of labour clauses which, according to the ILO Convention 94, are not covered by them. New in the re-submission of the resolution is the fact that the Ministry of Employment issued a circular on working clauses in public contracts in the summer of 2014, but this does not change the fact that the proposal is detrimental to competitiveness and the Danish economy. Instead of looking at the figures, we can see that the 3F report shows that a large part of the country's municipalities can of their own accord find a way of applying work clauses without interfering with it. That is why I find no reason why we in this House should decide to legislate on behalf of everyone. We in the Left have confidence in the social partners. We have confidence in the Danish model and we do not need to make demands on companies about what salary their employees should have, how working hours should be etc. etc. We do not believe that we should legislate out of everything, and that is why the Left is voting against the motions for resolutions. Thank you.

And it was needed because 10 years of a bourgeois government was one long desert migration in relation to the fight against social dumping. It was not on the agenda, it was not a priority. So there's enough to get to grips with, and it takes a lot of bets and a long cool move. Fortunately, we are strong forces, even here today, who are willing to do so. And we have increased the use of work clauses in municipalities and regions. This is good because a strong trade union movement is a unique and indispensable weapon in the fight against social dumping. But like I said, it doesn't stop here. The fight against social dumping continues and it is absolutely crucial that we continue to have a labour market based on the Danish model. Both of the motions for resolutions we are debating now aim to make it compulsory to introduce working clauses when all public authorities conclude contracts. As it is today, only state authorities are obliged to enter into agreements on work clauses. For municipalities and regions, however, it is voluntary. And as the Minister has also explained well, there has been a great deal of progress in the last few years, compared to how seriously it is taken in municipalities and regions. In other words, voluntary schemes have actually begun to have an effect. This is really, really pleasing, because if we're going to go along with this long, tough move, it's crucial that there's also ownership in municipalities and regions. It brings some local attention. It creates some local commitment to ensuring good pay and working conditions, and that is what we see the effect of now. I think we can get very far along the road of volunteering. I am glad to see that, unlike four years ago, the number of municipalities taking this seriously has increased. And that is absolutely crucial, because otherwise it will be just words on a piece of paper. I think that is at least now the most effective way to go. I am glad that municipalities and regions have got their way. I am glad that we are now seeing an increase all around. ParlaMint-DK_20150428170533	Thank you, President. These two proposals we are discussing today are, after all, almost identical to those we have discussed before. This does not mean that we do not legislate, because we do, but it means that we are careful to legislate. And we have regulated a number of rules in the area of health and safety, for example, and we have done so in consultation with the parties. Here we have jointly assessed the need for legislation to ensure the health and safety of workers. But when it comes to pay and working conditions, we have a great tradition that these are arranged between trade unions on the one hand and employers on the other. At the same time, it is the duty of the parties to ensure that these conditions are respected. That is how we want it to be in the future both because it is the least bureaucratic way and because we think it is the most effective way to regulate pay and working conditions in Denmark. This is also the most effective way of preventing social dumping in the long term. If we make a lot of complicated rules, we are not just undermining the Danish model; no, we are also risking that it is the big companies and only those who can understand the rules and who can handle the extra bureaucracy that will keep up with the extra costs. So we will have a situation where small businesses cannot compete with large companies when public tasks are being offered, and at the same time we risk putting Danish companies at a disadvantage compared to their foreign competitors. The third argument: it is an intervention in the Danish model. The argument is that if we use legislation to impose these working clauses on municipalities and regions, then we must also legislate on the level of wages. It is an intervention in the Danish model, and we will not, and that is why we do not want to be involved in it. It's a glorious circle that's been made there. But I'm sorry, it doesn't add up. Why is it an intervention in the Danish model if we are legally requiring regions and municipalities to use work clauses, but it is not an intervention in the Danish model when we are requiring the state and state companies to do so? There is nothing in the ILO Convention or in the Danish Order about it that demands should be made for very specific pay and working conditions. The Ministry's circular recommends that reference be made to the most representative agreements, and we have to assume that when the parties have entered into an agreement it is because they have the necessary clarity in terms of how to pay them. It is a system on which we agree, in the state field and for state enterprises. So there is no reason at all for this to be a trickle out of the hat. If we now impose on the regions and municipalities that they have to apply work clauses, then suddenly we have to go all the way down to crowns and ears indicating what the salary should be. It is also very interesting that the trade union movement does not therefore have a view that it would be contrary to the Danish model if we implemented the Enhedslisten's proposal here today. On the contrary, the LO and the FTF want it to be compulsory for all public authorities to apply work clauses under this convention we are talking about. The two organisations are not concerned with whether this should be done through legislation or through some sort of agreement between the authorities, trade union movement and government, or how it should now be combined, but they are of the opinion that it should be compulsory, and they are certainly not of the opinion that it should be an interference with the Danish model if it is implemented in the way that the Enhedslisten is proposing here. Behind it all, of course, is the argument about EU law: it is EU law that dictates that if we make any legislation, then we also have to legislate on the level of wages. However, that argument has been put down by the Minister himself through the answer I have received to my question 183, because here the Minister confirms that there are no EU judgments that say that if we impose on regions and municipalities the application of the provisions of the ILO Convention, then we must also legislate on the level of wages and that the amount of wages must then be in line with this Posting Directive, which dictates that we should not demand more than minimum wage and overtime payments for foreign service providers. ParlaMint-DK_20150428181515	Thank you to Mrs Mai Mercado. There are no more brief comments. We move on to the rapporteur for the authors of the motion. It's sir. Finn Sørensen.
ParlaMint-DK_20150428181529	First of all, I would like to thank Mrs Mai Mercado very much for taking up the substance of this matter. So there is enough reason to just outline how it is actually intended that such chain responsibility should work and what effect it should have. After all, the main contractor, the main provider of a service, can be made financially responsible for what has happened further down the chain, all the way down the last link of the chain. That's what it's all about. What effect will this have? This will have the effect that it will be a competitive advantage for all the companies that have order in the papers, all the companies that pay their taxes and their VAT and their social contributions on time, and all the companies that pay the wages they have now agreed with their employees. It will be a competitive advantage for those companies, and that is, I believe, the majority of small Danish craft enterprises. They're companies like that. This would be a major competitive advantage for them, because it would mean that if such general legislation were to be drawn up on chain liability, all main contractors would think more than once when they hired subcontractors and subcontractors. They would then secure themselves in the contracts through e.g. a guarantee that there was cover, that the rules had been complied with all the way down the chain. And that would be an advantage for the companies that have order in the papers. That's what it's all about. A general law on chain liability will really separate the sheep from the goats on the Danish labour market. I hope that this argument will convince Mrs Mai Mercado because I have responded to her concerns about it. If I cannot convince her by that, it is, of course, possible to obtain the suspicion that is also evident in relation to other rapporteurs who have been up here, that she actually has the view that there should be no regulation under these conditions. It's gonna be like the Liberal Alliance's, uh, sir. Joachim B. Olsens says, be free competition too, free competition where you don't pay tax and don't pay a proper salary or don't pay the wage that has been agreed. So that's the core of this whole case. How can this be done practically? It's not that complicated. In fact, the construction has already developed a good model for how to secure as a main supplier or supplier with subcontractors against unreasonable losses. There is, in fact, something called the building guarantee system in the building industry, I think it is still called, where it is necessary to obtain a certain amount of contract, that a guarantee sum, which can then be written off on a regular basis as the quality of the building is found to be in order, is made available as a supplier or contractor. The same model can of course be introduced in relation to chain liability, which is also about wages and taxes and social contributions. So it's not that complicated. Such arrangements exist in a number of other EU countries, and it has obviously not caused the huge fights to get there, so why should it be so difficult at home? Then there is the discussion over and over again that it will then be an intervention in the Danish model. It's some kind of construction you've created there, so you have an excuse to stop participating in it. It is clearly described in the proposal that this should be the salary now agreed with the employees. There is no need to legislate on this. So I would just like to refer to the fact that no one has really used it, but it is, as it were, still a little underlying in the Minister's arguments at least, perhaps also in the Socialist Group that this may well cause some problems in relation to EU law. The Socialist rapporteur would certainly not give a clear answer to the question. I just have to say that there is no reason for these concerns, because there is, after all, a so-called enforcement directive which clearly states that it is perfectly legal for the Member States to introduce chain liability. A very limited form of chain liability has been described in this enforcement directive, which cannot really be used for anything, but at the same time the directive states that Member States are free to apply more far-reaching schemes than those described in the directive, which also applies in other sectors. So there is no problem; it's kind of just about getting started. I think that all the effort we have made with the government to combat social dumping shows that it is needed. When you look at how many companies are actually caught up in the fact that things are not right, there is a very good reason for introducing such legislation as we are proposing here. There we can point to the fact that SKAT has demanded payments of up to a few hundred million kroner in the course of their visits to the companies. ParlaMint-DK_20150428182250	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for Children, Gender, Integration and Social Affairs.
ParlaMint-DK_20150428182313	Thank you, President. The Danish People's Party will with B 73 require the government to make legislative proposals that adults working with e.g. people with development inhibition in institutions, in communities of residence and similar places, after consent, must submit an adult certificate. The certificate shall show that the person concerned has not been convicted under the criminal code for a number of specific criminal offences, including sexual offences. The motion for a resolution is inspired by the children's certificates. Such certificates are compulsory for sports associations, scout clubs, day-care institutions, etc. to catch up. They must do so before hiring or employing persons who must have direct contact with, or have permanent contact with, children under the age of 15 and who have the possibility of direct contact with them. The government fully agrees with the proposers on the importance of preventing, among other things, For the purposes of this Decision, the following definitions shall apply: The Government therefore believes that the intention behind the proposal is both sympathetic and important. But if we look at the proposal, we have to note that it is unclear on a large number of points. In addition, it is unclear whether the solution outlined is in line with the scale of the problem. We have asked in KL, and they have stated that they do not collect information in this area. Furthermore, KL is not immediately aware of specific examples of employees who have previously been convicted of criminal offences. All the unclear elements must be seen in the light of the fact that already today you can obtain or ask for consent to obtain criminal records and that with the supervisory reform, we have been given a tool to ensure managemental focus on working targetedly to prevent abuses in housing offers. That is why the government cannot support the proposal. As I said, the resolution is unclear on a number of points. That is why we do not know how extensive it really is. DF proposes to negotiate that the proposal not only covers sexual crimes, but may also include, for example, sexual crimes. violence and theft. So we don't know what to actually include in an adult certificate. It is also not clear how long DF imagines that the information should be on the adult certificate. Is it for a shorter period, or e.g. to the registered person's 80th birthday? Nor is it clear whether the proposal covers persons already employed and working with the target group or whether it is aimed solely at new recruitments. If the proposal covers persons already employed, it will update a number of employment law issues, for example: the consequences of the employment law if a staff member refuses to consent to obtaining the adult certificate. Nor is it clear whether the proposal includes any volunteers who are established among the residents and help the residents but who are not paid for the effort. Finally, I have also noted that the DF has not indicated in the motion for a resolution how it should be financed. So it's also unclear. All these uncertainties must be seen in the light of the fact that, firstly, the government has already taken initiatives to prevent the target group from being subjected to sexual abuse and other abuses. The initiatives of the supervisory reform, which entered into force in 2014, shall contribute to ensuring that staff in the boforms covered by the supervision do not commit attacks against the target group. The Social Protection Agency shall decide on the approval of tenders on the basis of an assessment of whether the 24-hour offer is of the requisite quality. Quality is assessed on the basis of an overall assessment of a number of themes which are concrete in quality criteria. One of the criteria is that the offer prevents abuse, including both physical and psychological and sexual violence between citizens in the offer, abuse by citizens against employees and abuse by employees against citizens. The approval is a condition for the 24-hour offer to be included in the municipal supply. The target group referred to in the motion for a resolution is weak citizens, and it is precisely for this reason that supervision has strengthened the focus of the heads of 24-hour services on preventing violence between all citizens and employees. It is a responsibility that the leaders must take on, and it is a responsibility on which supervision is measured. As far as I am concerned, supervision and quality work are a very good instrument for working targetedly to prevent abuse in everyday life. The offer must focus on preventing violence every day and not only occasionally in connection with staff recruitment. Secondly, it is already an opportunity to obtain criminal records. The rules relating to the criminal record and criminal records belong to the Ministry of Justice, which has informed me that there are several possibilities for obtaining criminal records and decisions on criminal offences, the law on drugs and the restraining order. As the rules are today, it is possible for private and public authorities, with consent, to obtain private criminal records containing information on criminal offences which may be relevant to those who work with the disabled. Decisions will be based on the private criminal record for 2, 3 or 5 years, depending on the penalty. It is also possible, in a number of cases, for public authorities to obtain, with or without consent, public criminal records which also contain information on, inter alia,: Criminal offences. On a public record, the decisions are based on 10 years. Thus, both public and private employers today have the opportunity to obtain or request consent to obtain a criminal record where appropriate. From this point of view, I believe that the proposal here creates a great deal of automatic and, in other words, unnecessary bureaucracy. New types of criminal records and new cases for both public and private employers will therefore have to be developed. This must also be seen in the light of the fact that I am quite sure that all the parties that are in here today have so much advocated debureaucratisation. Finally, I would like to point out that with the rules we have for private and public criminal records today, a decision can be seen for a certain period of time. The rules reflect a balance between, on the one hand, considerations of inter alia: the citizens and, on the other hand, the opportunity for rehabilitation that we, as a society, should give to those who have endured a punishment. For example: a theft committed in youth many years ago prevent a person who has endured his or her punishment and has embarked on another life course from working with people with disabilities many years later? The Ministry of Justice has informed me that there is currently work going on in the Ministry of Justice to look more generally at what role the criminal records should play in the future and whether the balance in terms of rehabilitation should be adjusted. I believe that this is the right way to deal with the ideas expressed in the motion for a resolution, rather than creating different types of criminal record tests for different specific areas and where special rules should apply for catching up and application. In conclusion, I would like to reiterate that the government fully agrees with the intention of the proposal, but it is already possible to obtain or ask for consent to obtain criminal records. With the Social Protection Agency, we focus on preventing abuse and, as I said, work is currently being done in the Ministry of Justice to look more generally at what role the criminal records should play in the future. It is therefore very unclear whether the proposal, which even involves bureaucracy, is in line with the problem. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428191216	Thank you for that, President. People with disabilities have a significant risk of being subjected to sexual abuse. This is the conclusion of the literature study • Sexual abuse of people with disabilities, which is a review of foreign literature from 1980 to 2001 on the subject. A large proportion of the sexual assaults on people with disabilities are committed in the exposed's own surroundings at the settlement, day offer or elsewhere. In the vast majority of cases, the perpetrator is one who the person knows in advance, and the abuses are rarely reported to the authorities. Both adults and children and young people who are mentally handicapped must be protected to such an extent against abuse, theft and the like. In this context, however, attention should also be paid to sections 78 and 79 of the Criminal Code. It provides for the possibility to waive the right to pursue a profession if the circumstances of the case justify an obvious risk of abuse of the profession. These provisions are reported to be applied to a lesser extent to persons who have committed certain offences against, for example, the elderly or those with disabilities. There may also be cases of enrichment crime in which an employee steals from a resident in a settlement, which we have also seen flagrant examples of when it comes to the handicapped. The absence of justice means that the sentenced person must not take up work within the area for a period of time which varies depending on what he is convicted of. It's three years in the second one I read out, and in some cases it's five years. But that means that you will be able to work again in the area. There are three important comments on the existing regulatory framework. It is that the duration of 5 years may be insufficient. For example, in the case of sexual abuse, it should: be life-long. The provision is also said to be used to a modest extent, which may mean that there are some dark figures in the area. As I read out earlier, this is a 'can' clause which can be put into effect when issuing the criminal record; the Ministry of Justice is not aware that in this area this is the case in practice everywhere. I think that these factors, which are relevant in this area, underpin the proposal. A certificate, not least the employer's obligation to obtain it, could significantly strengthen the prevention of this type of abuse, etc. Therefore, I would also refer to international research in the form of a literature study from 2001, sexual abuse of people with disabilities, published by the SUS. It states that for people with disabilities there is a significant risk of sexual abuse. Developers are assumed to be at particular risk, partly because of the nature of their disability and partly because of the context in which they live in institutions where the abuses can be committed by several. For the handicapped, there is also a serious risk of being exposed to theft, fraud or the like. There are no actual studies, but Berlingske has run a number of articles from 2011, which have provided a number of examples. It is also obvious that consideration should be given to whether criminal offences other than sexual abuse should be included in the certificate. This must then be seen in the context of the concrete assessment under the criminal code. It may be that not all criminal offences are involved, but only those cases where a person has a job in which care is given to people with disabilities. For example, all cases where theft has been carried out by employees in this type of offer and institution should perhaps be adopted, but perhaps there is no need to include other enrichment crimes committed in other contexts. It's a balancing act. That is what I want to discuss with the other rapporteurs. We haven't seen a large number of these cases, but there are still a few a year. As I said earlier, I believe that there is a certain amount of darkness in this area. The cases are divided into two main categories: sexual abuse and theft and fraud. In both types of cases, the perpetrator is sentenced, in addition to imprisonment or fines, to the so-called disqualification of rights, which means that you cannot work within the area for a period of 2 to 5 years, but subsequently you can work in the area again. On our proposal, I also think that we are thinking, among other things. can talk about sexual abuse, including sexual intercourse or anything else, and that we can decide that if it has happened, one simply must have deprived the right to work with the most vulnerable in our society forever. Furthermore, I am also thinking that our proposal will increase the likelihood that employers will check certificates and obtain criminal records, because I do not think that is happening everywhere today. I have previously worked as a social and health worker and social and health assistant, also in home care, and have seen and heard that some have committed theft or emptied a resident's bank account and later been employed in private home care or other. ParlaMint-DK_20150428191946	Thank you for the floor, President. I fully agree with that. In the motion for a resolution, the Danish People's Party will require the government to put forward a bill banning so-called chain-cousin marriages, so that the children of parents who are cousins may not marry a cousin themselves. The Danish People's Party justifies the motion for a resolution by saying that there is an increase in the health risks to children of parents who are related and that the proposal will promote integration. I recognise that there may be health-care challenges associated with chain-cousin marriages and cousin marriages at all. In this context, I would like to stress that the government naturally agrees that, for the sake of children, attempts should always be made to minimise health risks. When blood relatives, including cousins and cousins, have children together, there are increased health risks for the child. However, if you look at it factually, it is the case that the health authorities have indicated that this alone is a relatively small increase in health risks. In many other situations there may be a corresponding increase in health risks. This applies, for example, when the child becomes a test tube fertilization or when a woman becomes pregnant in the 40s. The health authorities have indicated that the average risk of having children in a chain-cousin marriage does not mean, from a purely medical point of view, that reproduction should be discouraged. Instead, good information, good advice and good grief should be provided. It is therefore a question of providing medical information and help to the cousins who want to marry and have children together. Having said that, a ban will also be difficult to regulate, as there will be a great risk of circumvention. For example, it will. be difficult in the case of birth certificates, baptism certificates or the like to prove to the marriage authority that one is not a cousin or a cousin. And it will be even more difficult to prove that your parents are not cousins and cousins. It is the government's view that all citizens in Denmark have the right to enjoy the same basic rights, and this applies among other things. the right to choose one's spouse. Therefore, it is the government's view that if cousins choose to marry each other and plan to have children, we must make sure that we do everything possible to ensure that they make their decision on an informed, health-care basis. This means that they know the risks associated with having children with blood relatives. It is not up to the state to impose a ban here. People must be allowed to choose for themselves, but I clearly see it as a state task to ensure that this is an enlightened choice. This must and must be achieved through health education and prevention. The free choice is important when entering into marriage, and the Danish marriage authorities must already ensure that this is not a matter of forced marriage. When we then turn to a specific group and talk about integration and, in this context, about the fight against forced marriages, social control and other honour-related conflicts, I would say that the government's national strategy against honour-related conflicts contains targeted efforts, which include: must help to ensure that no one marries against his will. And that is precisely where the real integration effort in this area lies. For if we are to effectively counteract forced marriages and social control, we must change the mindset that exists in some families, where it ends with involuntary marriages and, in this case, forced marriages. There should be no doubt whatsoever that the government wants these outdated traditions to be put to good use, but a ban on chain-cousin marriages will not strengthen integration or the fight against social control. A ban with the stated justification risks maintaining some in the view that Danish society particularly wants to restrict the rights of new dancers. When we say that young people who have to marry must be able to decide for themselves, it must also go the other way: that we should not regulate who people are going to marry. Thus, the health risks to children of parents who are chain-cousin spouses must be addressed through information and prevention. When you marry, it must be done according to an informed choice, so we must prevent and work on attitudes, as I have mentioned a number of times. That's the long, tough move, but we're on our way. Nor will a ban strengthen integration or prevent forced marriages. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428194239	The proposers propose a ban on cousin-cousin marriages for generations in order to reduce the health risks of family growth and to strengthen the integration of these immigrant families, and such a practice is a cultural norm. In SF we acknowledge that there may be health and integration problems associated with chain-cousin marriages. The Danish Health Authority has shown evidence that cousin-cousin compounds lead to a statistically increased risk of stillbirth, infant death and congenital malformations, where there is a doubling of the risk compared to children whose parents are not related. However, this is a very small increase which, overall, corresponds to other situations where the mother, for example, is in the same situation. has diabetes, and in such situations it is considered perfectly acceptable to have a child. So if we are to ban cousin marriages with the argument that it is dangerous to health, we should also ban mothers with diabetes to have children, and I do not think anyone in this Chamber is interested in that. We actually believe that all citizens in Denmark have the right to enjoy the same basic rights, including the right to choose their own spouse. Nor should it be the responsibility of the state to decide who to marry and have children with. Rather, the state should help to ensure that the decision to enter into a cousin marriage is based on an informed choice so that they know what is of health risks. It is therefore necessary to look more closely at how health services can contribute to informing and preventing health problems. In relation to the integration issue, we agree that there may be a danger that cousin-cousin marriages may be an expression of social control and forced marriages. However, there are already initiatives which try to prevent this. In the government's national strategy, there are already targeted efforts that take up the fight against social control, to ensure that no one marries against their own will. At the same time, with these preventive measures, we are sending out a signal that forced marriages are totally unacceptable. We do not believe that a ban could strengthen integration or the fight against social control, but rather push new dancers further away from Danish society and Danish values. This will maintain some in the view that Danish society will restrict the rights of new Danish citizens, and it will also create a larger ones and us, which we will not go very far with in terms of integration. Furthermore, it will also be difficult to document who is cousin and cousin, so that the ban can easily be circumvented. The law will thus ultimately be of no use; it will only create a greater gap between new dancers and Danish society. For these reasons, we do not believe that the ban is a good solution, so the SF cannot support it.

And while it is true that very few Danes today marry their cousins or cousins, there is no doubt that cousin-cousin marriages were in the past quite widespread, including in Western Europe. Charles Darwin and Albert Einstein were both married to their cousin, and if we look under domestic skies, it is widely known that for example. Heir Prince Knud was married to her cousin, Princess Caroline-Mathilde. In addition, if you are a little interested in genealogical research, most people and probably many in this assembly will find cousin-cousin marriages among their own ancestors. The Danish cultural standard is therefore of recent date, and the practice that can be observed in defined immigrant communities is thus not significantly different from that which a few generations back had had great and accepted distribution at home. It is true and well known that there is an increased risk of cousin-cousin parents giving birth to malformed children. It is estimated that the risk is twice as high when the parents are cousins and cousins than when the parents are not related. It may sound violent, but the vast majority of cousins will have healthy children. At the same time, it is worth noting that the risk of malformations due to cousin cousin co-life is significantly lower than the risk is in an unrelated relationship, if the mother has diabetes, or where fertilisation has been done by the test tube method. Nevertheless, we probably all consider pregnancy and childbirth to be perfectly acceptable choices in the examples mentioned. The Danish People's Party is considering how their proposals can be implemented in practice. The model doesn't look very clear. It is mentioned, among other things, that future husbands must sign a declaration of faith and law that they are not themselves the children of parents who are cousins, that the cpr register must include the possibility for the authorities to check whether the parents of married couples are cousins and that couples who want to marry must themselves document a lack of kinship with the authorities. In the ears of the Liberal Alliance, it sounds much like more monitoring, heavier bureaucracy and difficult controllable arrangements with problematic legal consequences. When we sum up, none of the arguments of the Danish People's Party are really convincing. In the Liberal Alliance, therefore, we cannot support the proposal. We would rather maintain the freedom that exists today and, moreover, aim to ensure that information and social security are the way forward.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428201503	I would like to thank the Left, the Conservative and Liberal Alliance for the question which allows me to explain and deepen government policy in this area. After all, this is not the first time we have discussed whether the economic incentive to be in jobs rather than receiving public support is strong enough. Therefore, it should not come as a surprise that the government is concerned that it should pay to work. That is not only something we say, it is actually something we have done something about. After all, it is no secret that the possibility should arise, we are happy to continue in the path that we put in with the 2012 tax reform. Because, of course, it is important that there is an economic benefit from being in a job rather than being on public support. And that's too far for the vast majority of people today. As the tax reform is fully phased in, the economic incentive to be in employment is further strengthened. In particular, the tax reform strengthens the economic incentives to be in employment for people with relatively weak incentives today. This is because the reductions in tax reform are targeted at low and middle incomes. With the tax reform, the employment allowance is almost doubled and it is more than tripled for single parents. The subdued regulation of transfer incomes, which is part of the reform, also helps to strengthen the economic incentive. As an example of the positive effects of the reform, I can mention that the group with a so-called net compensation rate above 80%. will be significantly reduced from 336,000 people in 2011 to 249,000 people when the tax reform is fully phased in. Net rates of compensation have filled a great deal in the debate on economic incentives for employment, and this may sound a bit like an economist, but it is nevertheless a simple expression of how much disposable income you have as a transfer recipient represents of the disposable income you have if you are employed. Persons with a net level of compensation over e.g. 80% 90% or 90%. is usually said to have a relatively weak economic incentive to be in employment rather than receiving a transfer income. And these groups are the ones that get significantly smaller. I should not hide the fact that I think it is gratifying if we now strengthen the economic incentives, because the stronger the economic incentives for employment are, the more there will be in jobs, and therefore it is important that we constantly consider how to strengthen them. But things have to be balanced. Further strengthening of economic incentives must be done in a socially balanced way. We, as politicians, must also be careful not to get too one-sided a focus on economic incentives; a focus that, in my opinion, often characterizes the presentation of those who have raised the question today. Of course, economic incentives play an important role, but that is not the only thing that determines whether or not a person is in employment. Other relationships are also involved. On the TV paper here at 5:00 a.m. At 18.30 you could see one of the many Danes who have come to work lately while waiting to come down here in the Parliamentary Chamber, where we have today welcomed new employment figures which show that it has now been 22 months in a row with employment in the private sector. And when the welder lifted the mask to speak to the TV newspaper, he said that you feel that you are contributing to society. That is certainly true because he felt that way at least, and in any case it is a good expression that it is not only financial incentives that make people want to take a job. This is the case, for example. the satisfaction of contributing actively in the labour market, the community of work colleagues and the importance of being able to support oneself and one's family. If, as politicians alone, we focus on the economic gain, we risk leaving the impression that it is okay to say no to a job if you think that the economic gain is too small. But it's not okay. We have arranged our society in a way where we have a high level of compensation, that where, in return, everyone has the right and obligation to support themselves, if they can, and that applies regardless of what they have the opportunity to obtain transfer income and other public services. That is why we have the obligation to provide benefits and cash benefits, and that is why the rules on availability must also be enforced effectively and consistently. If you can work, you have to be available for the labour market. This is a guiding principle in the whole way we have built the welfare society. It should also be borne in mind that even if a unemployed person only has a small financial gain from getting into a job here and now, getting a foot in the labour market can open up new opportunities. With a larger network in the labour market and more work experience in luggage, it will often be possible to get a new and better paid job, and in a new and better paid job the benefits of being in employment will, by their very nature, be higher. That is another reason why, in the debate, we should not legitimise the fact that we are not available for the labour market if we think that the economic benefit of taking a job is just too small. When it comes to forward-looking initiatives that can bring more people into work, a broader perspective can also be taken than that of today's question. The economic incentive to be in employment rather than to receive e.g. cash benefits or cash benefits are certainly a relevant piece. But it is only one piece among several that we can move on when it comes to the effort to raise the supply of labour and increase employment. In the coming years, we will see that employment is increasing as the series of reforms that have been implemented is fully phased in. This applies, in addition to the tax reform, among other things. reforms of retirement, early retirement, flexible jobs, the SU system and international recruitment. One of the major tasks I see for us in the coming years is therefore also to follow up on whether the many reforms that have already been implemented will also have the effect that we had expected and, if necessary, we will have to intervene and adjust if there are elements that do not seem to work as intended. Whether the ongoing work of following up the reforms qualifies as an initiative in itself can be discussed, but in any case the importance of following up on the initiatives we have launched should not be underestimated. The government has presented an ambitious reform agenda, on which we are continuously following up, and the work has not been done with the reforms that we have already implemented. That is why I think we should continue to look into and consider the possibilities for implementing new initiatives to increase employment. I would like to invite you to debate and dialogue on this. There is no reason to sit around and hold on to the good ideas. If you have new ideas that can contribute to an increase in employment, it is simply to put them open and freely forward for discussion, so that it can be seen whether you meet at least the demands we make. Our demand is that these are not proposals for their own sake. They must be concrete; we must be sure that they work as intended; and then they must be socially balanced and fully funded.

ParlaMint-DK_20150428202218	Thank you, Minister, for your reply. Basically for the Left and in the DNA of the Left, you have to get a win when you make a bet. This, which some people regard as indifference, whether you get a prize or not, whether it matters whether you make an effort or not, is very difficult for us. We think it is important that you animate that you make an effort at all stages of life, and that you can then expect a carrot in the form of a gain when you have achieved a good result. This is also true of those in the labour market. It is important that we have as many people as possible on the labour market, it says just like itself, and I think everyone agrees on that, but it is also important that we have a framework and some systems that make it animate that efforts are also being made, make an effort to get the work that there is, move a little longer for it than might otherwise have been desired, etc. We can see that there is apparently a majority of Danes who agree with us if you are to believe the measurement that Jyllands-Posten brought here the other day. I think that is actually quite natural for the Danes to feel that way. There has to be a gain by making a difference. Of course, it is also a question of getting up in the morning and having something relevant to stand up for, getting to the workplace, having a social community both at the workplace, but perhaps also in leisure time. But the problem is that the proverb does not work enough in the real world. When you are looking for labour for a typical low-wage job, the situation is that you rarely get applicants, at least not Danish applicants. This applies to the food industry, to the hotel and catering industry, to a number of areas. When you search for labour in the low-wage areas, you rarely get very many, if any, Danish applicants. It is the case that one in five Danes of working age is on transfer income for various reasons. Barely 800,000 of working age are on transfer income. It costs society in the vicinity of 173 billion. kr. a year. And the paradox is also obvious. We currently have some 40,000 foreigners, typically from Eastern Europe, who are working in Denmark. For many, it is low-wage work, it is unskilled work, and at the same time we have just below the bottom, but close to the same figure, 40,000 Danes on cash benefits, for whom the only challenge is that they do not have a job. We think that is a paradox that should have been addressed. If you compare the situation in Denmark with, for example, Sweden, it is the case that in Denmark there are three and a half to support you on transfer income. In Sweden, the five-and-a-half working people share that burden. Of course, one might ask: is it attractive to take a job, move so far, go for the jobs that are? According to the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Interior, 330,000 have what is called a poor financial incentive to work and even after we have phased in the tax reform, which we are also facing together with the government, in 2023, there are still some 250 000 people left for whom there is little economic incentive to work. We think that we can do better, and unfortunately we have to note that, despite the tax reform, the government has made it more attractive to be outside the labour market, in the sense that the ceiling on cash benefits has been removed, the 225-hour rule has been removed, the entitlement to more holidays has been given to cash benefits, and the start-up aid has been removed. This has all meant that, for example, a family on cash benefits has received in the vicinity of 3,000 DKK. more a month. A refugee family has received an increase of about 10,000 kroner. a month. We in the Left want a job reform, a job reform that must make the difference between being on the labour market and outside the labour market greater. We want a ceiling to be set on the overall amount of public benefits, and then we want to lighten the tax on small labour income, simply to increase the benefit of working. We need more income for our common welfare society, and we point to this model as a way of getting more of the around 800,000 working age into the labour market, which is now outside the working community. Every time we move just one person on cash benefits into the labour market, it gives the economy a plus of DKK 175,000. in addition to the social gain that lies with the individual. We think we should have done something about this, and we hope that the debate here will reflect this, regardless of what an election result might look like, and that we can then work together and move forward in that direction. Against this background, on behalf of the Left, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservative People's Party, I should like to make the following points: Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament notes that, according to the Ministry of Finance, in a reply to the Finance Committee on 27 November 2013, 17,700 people would be made available by being on public support than by working. The Danish Parliament also notes that, according to the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Home Affairs, there are still 250,000 Danes who have little financial incentive to work when the government's tax reform is fully phased in in in 2023. Parliament therefore calls on the government to take initiatives that will ensure that there is always an economic benefit by taking a job rather than being on public support. The Folketing also invites the government to look at the total benefit that a family can receive in public benefits, including considering the introduction of a cash benefits ceiling, as well as reintroducing the start-up benefit. • V 42).

The truth is that if you want to honour your promise that it must always pay to work, then the party must again cut the daily allowance because it is almost exclusively entitled to benefit, as well as to note for the vast majority of people in jobs that are currently part of the group that does not have an immediate benefit from working. Then you have to secure it, you have to do as the Left's friends in the CEPOS say. Then the daily allowance should be reduced by about 10%. As a Social Democrat, I can clearly say that this is not a road we want to take, on the contrary. We want an unemployment benefit system where fewer than today fall out and security is greater, where we ensure that more people get to work at a decent wage and where we focus any tax cuts on those who have the lowest wages. For us, the answer is never one-sidedly hollowing out the social safety net. That is not the way for the Denmark we know.

ParlaMint-DK_20150429235922	Thank you.
