The debate on the EU-US free trade agreement and the consequences of the so-called investor-state dispute settlement system, a little more popularly called the ISDS mechanism, are being conducted in many places. In fact, it is a growing discussion and concern that is around in relation to the very far-reaching rights granted to investors at the expense of the opportunities for democratic regulation in national parliaments. A number of national parliaments have actually made very critical statements about ISDS. This is among other things. In the Netherlands and France, and in the Austrian Parliament, the official position has been taken that the ISDS should be completely removed from the agreement. The European Parliament is currently discussing what position Parliament should take on the agreement. In this context, a large number of committees have made a great deal of criticism of the ISDS mechanism. For example, the Employment Committee, the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Policy, the Committee on Legal Affairs and the Committee on Constitutional Affairs have all called for the ISDS to be completely removed from the agreement. But criticism does not just come from national parliaments. The European Commission's ISDS hearing produced 150,000 responses. In fact, it is the largest number of consultation responses ever received by the European Commission. Of the answers, 97 percent demanded that ISDS be withdrawn from the agreement. It is not only in Europe that ISDS meets criticism. This is also happening at home in Denmark. The Committee on European Affairs has today received critical requests from, among others, the Commission. environmental organisation NOAH and Africa Contact. Today's debate is therefore important. I think it is important that we take these critical voices seriously. ParlaMint-DK_20150429000304	Thank you for that and thank you for the question. Well, it's like Mr. Kim. Nikolaj Villumsen said an investor state dispute settlement scheme we are going to talk about in the new free trade agreement between the European Union and the United States. I was actually promised a beer if I could come up with a joke about ISDS, and then I thought, "What's the difference between a giraffe and ISDS?" Yeah, a giraffe, you know what it is. It may as well not trigger a beer, but it was an attempt to cheer us up a little at this time of night. I will, of course, try to answer the question, and I will do so by answering four questions and then making a small conclusion. I'll try to be brief. The first question is: why is it important for us to have a free trade agreement between the European Union and the United States? It is for four reasons. Firstly, because it creates economic growth and increased exports for the benefit of Danish companies. Secondly, because it will result in lower prices for a wide range of consumer goods which are important for Danish families. Thirdly, because it is important to have a trade agreement with the United States so that the United States can be a much more important market for the European Union and for Denmark, at a time when we are really up against competition with other markets around the world and trade agreements that are being concluded, not least in South America and Asia. Finally, fourthly, we want a free trade agreement between the European Union and the United States, because it must help to ensure high standards of world trade in terms of sustainability in terms of labour rights and in terms of environmental considerations. The second question I would like to answer is: why is it so reasonable for investment protection to be included in this trade agreement? This investment protection, this ISDS mechanism, we're talking about up here as if it's a whole new animal in the revelation that we've never seen before. Yes, we do. We have seen this for the last 50 years in trade agreements. We have 37 Danish trade agreements that include investment protection. The EU has 1,400. So it is a little strange that it is now suddenly being made a very, very strange thing, that you have investment protection in trade agreements, but nevertheless that is what is happening, and that is why we are discussing it. But why is it sensible for there to be investment protection? Yes, of course because we have to secure Danish companies and other companies from among others. discrimination and discriminatory treatment in markets and against the possibility of their investments being expropriated in some way. It is, I suppose, the main concern of such an agreement, so as to ensure fair and equal conditions of competition for companies in the markets covered by a trade agreement. Moreover, because it is important that when we make trade agreements, there is a level playing field for companies in the countries that they include. The third question is: should consideration of investors and protection of investors take precedence over the right of countries to make rules themselves, to make legislation and to set higher standards? No, it should not, and Denmark and many other EU countries will never accept a trade agreement in which investment protection will be arranged in such a way. It is also in the Commission's mandate, the Commission, which is negotiating this agreement on behalf of Denmark, that the right to regulate, the right to set standards itself, the right of countries to regulate before investment protection, must be the principle. The fourth and final question I would like to answer is: is there a need for changes to the existing investment protection rules that we know about? Yes, there is, and there is a good reason for that, because, as I said, they have been in existence for 50 years, and this has also proved to be the case, we can see in some of the cases that have been raised that there is too wide a margin of interpretation which provides an inappropriate balance between the consideration of the law of the States and the ability of companies to obtain compensation. So that room needs to be narrowed in. We have also seen, as the Enhedslisten's rapporteur stressed, that there has been a wide-ranging debate on this particular issue, and there have been 150,000 requests to the Commission, but the Enhedslisten's rapporteur forgot to mention that 97%. of them, or something like that, is such a thing where you have been on a website and marked in a box, and then it has gone down to the Commission. We know them well, but still. This is a matter of concern and the Commission has taken it seriously. I'll take that seriously. I also know that my fellow ministers take this seriously, and that has led, among other things, to the fact that we have not been able to do so. the debate and debate that have taken place with the European Parliament and the debate that has taken place with us ministers from the countries, that we are now working on four areas in which we want to improve the protection of investment. Like I said. Paragraph 1: We must be absolutely clear that the right to regulate takes precedence over investment protection. Paragraph 2: The composition of the arbitrations which determine these cases must be ensured so that the participants who are there, the judges who sit there, are impartial and can also be pointed out by national governments. Point 3: There must be a better link between decisions by national courts and international arbitration. Point 4: It is necessary to examine whether a form of appeal can be established after a case has been settled by ISDS. These are concerns that have been raised, and they are therefore concrete improvements that are now being worked on and that we expect the Commission to present proposals for shortly, in other words not until May. So that's how the system works. There has been a hearing that has raised a number of concerns. We take those concerns seriously. We're making improvements. Denmark is working for this, and on this basis I think it is very, very important that we in the Danish Parliament should decide to confirm our commitment, of course, to an important free trade agreement, which means growth and economic progress also for Denmark, but there must be investment protection in the trade agreement which is based on the principles I have mentioned here. Against this background, I look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-DK_20150429001242	The fundamental problem with ISDS is that it restricts the rule of the people and democracy in favour of large companies. If the ISDS becomes part of the agreement, it may reduce the possibilities of regulating for the future in the Folketing. In the past, ISDS has been used by multinational companies to prosecute states which raised the minimum wage, which wanted to dismantle nuclear power and which, for example, wanted to change the labelling of cigarette packets. The danger is, of course, that Denmark may be affected by its legal actions in the future, in which a company pursues its own interest at the expense of a democratic decision in the interest of the Community. It could be because we in the Folketing decided to ban a dangerous substance in baby toys to take care of our children, or it could be because we would introduce social clauses into all public procurement to stop social dumping • democratic decisions that could affect the earnings of international investors. ISDS gives companies the freedom to go to special courts bypassing our national legal systems in order to defend their own narrow corporate interests against wider public interests. In the Enhedslisten, we think this is the wrong way to go. The Minister for Trade and Development has great faith in the new and so-called modern approach to ISDS, which the European Commission, following the great criticism of the consultation process, has raised. But unfortunately, this optimism is not shared by the experts. Last Friday, we had a thematic meeting on the EU-US Free Trade Agreement in the Parliament's European Committee together with the Danish MEPs. Here, researcher Lauge N. Skovgaard told Poulsen, who among others. has worked for the English Government and Danish Industry on the question of TTIP and ISDS that the so-called modern ISDS, as we have already seen, for example, in the EU-Canada agreement, has not prevented the major problems that other countries have had in the past with ISDS in other agreements. In other words, it is modern in this case old wine in new bottles, which will not prevent Denmark from being confronted with major lawsuit from multinational companies if they believe that democratic decisions made in the Folketing mean that there is a loss of their expected earnings and that therefore they demand compensation. If you listen to the public debate and indeed the Minister's speech, it may sound as if ISDS is urgently needed. Yeah, it's kind of irresponsible if ISDS doesn't come as part of the deal. I would like to take the liberty of attacking a few of the myths that unfortunately flourish in the debate on ISDS. Firstly, there is real discrimination. After all, foreign investors are granted rights that national companies do not have. It's unfair competition. Secondly, investors are already protected. For example, if you are an investor, you can take out insurance on the private market or at the World Bank. Thirdly, there is reason to worry about legal proceedings against Denmark. Sometimes it is emphasised in the debate that we already have a lot of trade agreements with ISDS mechanisms, as the Minister, among other things. I was just talking about it. That is true, but the agreements are agreements that we have with developing countries and, as everyone knows, there are very few investments from developing countries in Denmark. Investment goes the other way, and that is why we have not been hit by legal proceedings. It was Canada in return when they entered into a free trade agreement called NAFTA with the United States and Mexico. At that time it was thought in Canada that only Mexico would be affected by such lawsuit from US multinationals. The reality was that it was to a large extent Canada that was affected by these proceedings. I think it is remarkable that there are no examples of investors from Denmark or the EU having problems in the United States who could have been solved with an ISDS mechanism. This is certainly not something that I have heard at this stage about or heard produced in the debate. So there is no basis for giving these very far-reaching rights to investors. Finally, it is said that this is important for example. to be able to make an agreement with China that you have ISDS with. In this context, I would just like to point out that China precisely wants an ISDS agreement in the investment agreement negotiated between the EU and China. So that is not an argument, but perhaps a concern that could be raised in the context of the agreement with China. That is not an argument for introducing it here in the EU-US agreement. In other words, the arguments for the far-reaching consideration of investors at the expense of the people's regime are not there. On the other hand, there is a massive criticism which I think we should take seriously. We should ensure that in the future there is an opportunity to regulate for the benefit of people and the environment. That is why we have a proposal from the Enhedslisten for adoption which, with the permission of the President, I would like to read out. It reads as follows: Proposal for adoption The Danish Parliament refers to the submission by the European Committee of the negotiating mandate to the EU-US Free Trade Agreement, which stated that the agreement should not have a negative impact on environmental and consumer standards or labour rights, urged the government to reject the inclusion of an ISDS scheme in the agreement. In addition, the Danish Parliament notes that the ISDS arrangements in other agreements have significantly reduced the right of States to regulate on everything from environmental, health and consumer standards to tax and labour rights, that the only way to guarantee the State's right to introduce laws and regulations in accordance with the public interest is by not introducing an ISDS scheme in relations with the United States, that the Commission's reformed approach to investor protection, as presented in the ISDS consultation process, does not ensure sufficient protection of the State's right to regulate and that the ISDS scheme has justified significant criticism from the trade union movement and environmental and consumer organisations on both sides of the Atlantic as well as from leading Member States, Germany and France. • V 43)

So, of course, you can make these agreements without the ISDS mechanism. What is important to note is that an ISDS mechanism is a very, very far-reaching right that you give to investors, where it is not just that you come and expropriate them, but where you can claim money for expected loss of earnings if, for example, you legislate for something here in the Folketing, it is about environmental protection or consumer rights that you then think you are losing money on as a company.

ParlaMint-DK_20150429005326	Thank you, sir. President, and thank you to the Enhedslisten for raising this debate on the formulation of the trade agreement between the EU and the United States. After all, the Socialists' wish with a trade agreement is to preserve and improve prosperity and prosperity in Denmark, and to fulfil that wish, and we must improve the framework for trade between the European Union and the United States. The challenge, of course, is that, as the country now stands, there are barriers to trade between the European Union and the United States, and some barriers are necessary, while other barriers are unnecessary. The necessary barriers are about guaranteeing the right to maintain and develop legislation that safeguards our level of protection in the fields of the environment, health and consumer protection and the protection of workers' rights, and we would like to maintain those barriers. The unnecessary barriers are the only ones that are detrimental to trade and that do not affect the level of protection, and we would like to help remove them. These were the initial general comments on a trade agreement, but now it is not the first time that trade agreement is being discussed here in the Parliamentary Assembly, and this is also something that is being debated extensively in the European Committee, and that is a good thing, because we need to achieve an agreement that, on the one hand, secures our companies' investments in the United States, and, on the other hand, ensures that we in Denmark and in the European Union secure our regulation against undermining the trade agreement. It is therefore gratifying that the European Commission has listened to the scepticism expressed and has sent the part of the trade agreement that we are discussing today, namely investor protection, to public consultation. Many consultation responses have been received and it is good that I should not interfere in the discussion on how they have arrived and in the follow-up report after the hearing the Commission notes that there are four areas where, in relation to the European-Canadian Trade Agreement model, there is a need for further work and clarification to find satisfactory solutions to the key issues of investor protection. The four issues which the Commission will work to clarify are as follows: the first is that the right of States to regulate cannot be affected or restricted by investor protection. The second is that the establishment and functioning of the arbitration tribunals ensure that they are impartial and independent of private interests. The third is to ensure a balance between national courts and an international investment charge so that democratic deficits are eliminated. And the fourth and last thing the Commission is looking at is that we should examine whether an appeal body could be set up, where possible erroneous decisions from an arbitration tribunal could be dealt with. Following on from the thorough work of the European Commission, I find this proposal for adoption which the rapporteur of the Left recently read out quite comprehensively for the position of the Folketing, namely that the Folketing expresses the need for further improvements in the mechanism of EU investment protection agreements, and I welcome the fact that intensive work is now being done on it. With the scepticism that has been expressed in the public debate, it is important to me that we have a broad majority here in the Danish Parliament who listens to the concerns of the people and the desire for a trade agreement for the benefit of Denmark, for the benefit of Danish democracy, for the benefit of Danish companies, for the benefit of Danish citizens, also when they are consumers and when they are employees. In the text of the adoption, which was read out by the rapporteur of the Left, we write, and I quote: the Danish Parliament calls on the government to continue to work actively to ensure that civil society's criticism of the mechanism is met, so that it respects the EU and the Member States' right to maintain and develop the level of protection in legitimate policy areas such as the environment, health, consumer protection and labour rights.
